FOREWORD

Originating in Savoy, the Lanovazs have immigrated to every part of the world
This is the history of one family who came to Westem Canada in 1894, It is atale told
by their grateful descendants and traces the evolution of their families to the present time.

In such an undertaking, mistakes and omissions will inevitably occur and for this
we beg your forgiveness. In our defense, we can only say that we have done our very
best.

We hope that those who will leaf through this book will feel as much pride and

pleasure as we have had in putting it together.

AVANT-PROPOS

Originaires de la Savoie, les Lanovaz sont éparpillés at travers le monde. Ceci est
Vhistoire d'une famille qui, en 1894, vint sétablir dans !Ouest canadien. C'est un bref
compte rendu de nos ancétres et de l'évolution de leurs descendants,

Si des fautes ou des omissions se sont glissées dans le texte, nous nous en
excusons vivement et comptons sur votre compréhension. Nous zvons tout simplement
fait de notre mieux.

‘Nous espérons que vous aurez autant de plaisir & feuilleter ce bouquin que nous

en avons eu a le rédiger

ACKNOWLEDGEMENT

We would like to thank al family members who took the time to send us family
‘formation and pictures. Without your cooperation there would not be a family book

We also thank Mr. Gilbert Langlois in France who gathered all the information of
‘our early ancestors and was kind enough fo forward it to us

‘Thanks to our typist Lori Hirschkorn who had immense patiences with us, Last
but not least, thanks to all who helped to proof read, translate or help in all the other
ways. Weare grateful to all of you.

Compiling and editing was done by: Lucie Pogu, Clarence and Vivian Lanovaz,

Ida Périlat, Alice Perrin, Camille and Harvey Doucette, Solange Périllat, Thérése Lasiuk,

Pat Lanovaz, André Lanovaz and Arthur Périllat.

19

‘The following is a translation of notes on the Lanovaz family taken from town
archives and church registers by Gilbert Langlois of France, The biographical material on
the Frangois and Andréanne family was written by Marguerite Périllat of Duck Lake.

ARENTHON
A rural community consisting of 1147 hectares (3010 acres), a church, a castle, a
hamlet called Lanovaz and a small metal working factory. The population of the town in
1861 was 909 and in 1980 648,

THE CHURCH
Dedicated to Notre Dame, the choir is vaulted with a three panelled apse and

flying-buttresses from the outside. It is thought to have been built around the middle of|
the 15th Century. ‘The tower has been repaired on various occasions and in 1939 the
steeple was destroyed by fire. The church has a beautiful door dating from 1721, two
lateral walls dating from the 18th Century and a crypt in memory of Frangois Vanrier
"Restorer of this temple and father of the poor". By 1995 the church had been completely
restored,

THE CASTLE
Itdates from 1628 end originally belonged to the Faucigny-Lucinge Family. In
1705 it was given in marriage to Edouard de Conzie whose son Frangois Joseph de Conzie
des Charmettes was a friend of Jean Jacques Rousseau. It then passed on to the Gerbaix
de Sonnaz. family who occupied the chateau until 1921. At the entrance, in the alley,
stands the conciergerie, « renaissance pavilion (number 86 of France's "Monuments
Historiques"), The castle is presently divided into multi-family dwellings.

PAPER MILL
As early as the 15th Century there was a small paper mill in Arenthon, It
continued to function until about 1850 and at thet time employed four people

HISTORICAL NOTES

Since the early 1600 Arenthon and the hamlet of Lanovaz formed part of the
Duchy of Sayoie. Around 1713 this small state took the name of Realm of Sicily and in
1718 became the Kingdom of Sardinie. In 1792 France annexed the land, but it was
retrieved by the king of Sardinia in 1815, In 1860 it was retumed to France by
referendum,

The earliest Lanovaz records date from 1613. Some were written in French, some
in Latin and Lanovaz often spelled without the z

Church and Rectory in Arenthon, France

‘Church in Arenthon, France

Village Cranves-Sales where Maric Julie Goy was born
She married Gaspard Lanovaz in 1848 in this village
church.

21

ARENTHON AREA, HAUTE SAVOIE

The little village of Arenthon, a few kilometres north-east from La
Roche-sur-Foron nestles in the valley of the Arve, a river which joins the
Rhone and lac Leman near Geneva.

It |s one of the most fertile valleys of Savoie and blessed with 2
climate somewhat warmer than the rest of the country. Wheat, barley and
cats and some of the more hardy fruit are grown on small plots of land:
the dairy industry florishes and is home to some of the best cheese in
France. On the slopes vines are cultivated and the wine made is of an
excellent quality but hardly in quantities sufficient for export.

Life on the small farms in Savoie in the late nineteenth century was
harsh. Farms were small, the work entirely manual and at best supplied a
marginal subsistence. There was little room for expansion, the eldest son
usually inherited the farm, the other children had to fend for themselves.
The flourishing touristic industry of today was unknown. Savoie exported
its youth to the larger cities and to other countries. Many went to Algeria,
then a French colony, others looked to America, and particularly Western
Canada where land was to be had for almost nothing.

Badly informed of the hardships which they would face, few would have
stayed i f returning would have been possible. Having sold all their worldly
goods in order to raise enough money for their passage, they had little
choice but to stay, endure and fight.

This book is a tribute and the product of their glorious struggle. Never
before have so few fought so bravely against such formidable odds and
never again will comparable exploits be possible. They have carved for

themselves an everlasting place in the history of our country.

died there: Gaspard in 1863 and Marie Julie in 1893,

Jacques was taught Latin, Greek and Spanish by a private tutor. Later he became a

Redemptorist priest and was sent to South America to the Amazon region. He died there of
tropical fever.

Frangois and Andréanne (Genon) Lanovaz
‘Taken from a book written by Marguerite Périllat

Francois Lanovaz was a small farm owner in the hamlet
of Lanovaz near Arenthon, France. His interest in a
homestead in Canada was aroused by circulating brochures
nt by the Canadian Government. A fellow countryman, Mr.
Betemps who had been to California was praising the climate
he had seen there and teok for granted that all America must
enjoy that kind of weather. His wife, Andréanne did not
share in his enthusiasm. After receiving a letter from a
friend, Mr. Joseph Forestier, who had homesteaded in Canada
a few years before, telling how well he and his son Jean
Marie were doing with their farm and a livery barn they had
in Duck Lake, nothing would hold him back and that clinched
his decision to immigrate to Canada.

‘They sailed on the Sarnia Liverpool and after thirteen
and a half days landed in Halifax. The rest of the journey
was made by train. They arrived in Duck Lake at the end of
April, 1894 togather with their five children, Francoise 9,
Ferdinand 7, René 6, Julie 4, Amedine 2 and their fellow
companion Mr. Betemps.

Frangois, with his visions of rapidly becoming a well-
to-do land owner, looked alert and business like. His wife,
who had come reluctantly, locked scared and worried. She
had a vision of seeing painted Indians lurking close at
hand.

Their friend, Mr. Forestier, took them to his home. In
the meantime Francois and Mr. Betemps each bought a wagon
and a team of oxen and filed for homesteads in the Carlton
area some fourteen miles west of town. Frangois was very
fortunate in finding a two story log house for sale which
they partly dismantled to make it easier to move onto his
property.

When the house was ready the family moved in. There
were just trails to reach their place and they had to cross
the reserve which made Andréanne very nervous. After what
seamed an endless drive they entered a semi-circle clump of
thick bush and a nearby pond where stood the reconstructed
house. The children were elated and jumped with joy,
repeating a phrase Francoise had spontaneously composed, "Le
Canada, est le plus beau pays du monde. Déja nous
‘Canada is the most beautiful country in the
Already we love you.

Tt was spring but the soil was not ready for seeding.
They bought a few staples in Duck Lake but their diet was
mostly soup, bread and wild game. Andréanne had never made
bread but felt very lucky that Mr. Betemps was a skilled

50

Lanovaz Family 1903

BR. Julie, René, Frangoise, Ferdinand, F.R. Mother Adréanne &

Father, Frangois Lanovaz, Maric Louise, Helene, Marguerite, Joseph
Marcel was born in 1904

baker by trade. This saved her day. The two boys learned
how to catch rabbits and prairie chickens with snares made
out of leather shoe laces.

Their daughter Amedine passed away less than two months
after their arrival, then on August 4 of that year their
daughter Marie Louise was born with only the help of her
husband. Andréanne could not breast feed this baby and they
had no milk. A little flour with sugar and boiling water
was strained and put in a sterilized cloth and given to the
baby to suck. A little later a neighbor shared their cow's
milk with the baby. Today this baby girl is Aunty Marie
Louise Klassen, a healthy 101 year old lady.

Winter entertainment wae card playing but they had
failed to bring a deck from France. That did not deter
Ferdinand who gathering all the cardboard available made up
a complete deck. No wonder he later became a real card
shark!

Every summer, early settlers had been supplementing
their income by picking seneca roots. Francois and his two
eldest children went on a trek to Great Bear Lake with their
ox driven wagon, For that trip Andréanne made a hand seun
tent from some of their strong hemp sheets. Thay were gone
three weeks, one to travel and two for the digging. They
were pestered with black flies and mosquitoes and had to
make smoke smudges to protect themselves and their animals.
The following year the trip was made with horse and wagon

51

Andréanne was an accomplished designer and dress maker.
One day an elegantly dressed French man arrived at the
homestead asking her to make him a suit froma bolt of the
finest material. She could not pass the opportunity of
earning some money and so painstakingly sewed this complete
suit by hand.

In France, Christmas was strictly a religious holiday.
New Year's Day was the time for gifte and celebration.
Andréanne made mitts from worn-out woolen clothes and for
the girls she made each a dress out of pink and white
flowered bed canopy curtains she had brought from France.
There was no hope of ever using such a canopy in Canada.

one day in late December Andréanne and her son
Ferdinand left for town for a few Christmas supplies with an
Indian pony hitched to their homemade cutter. Darkness and
a blizzard overtook them on their return trip. They sensed
that their horse by instinct would take them home. They
suddenly came to a stop in front of a cabin. An alerted
Indian quickly took them inside, gave them tea and food and
attended to their horse. Andréanne's fear of Indians
vanished as she realized that the Chief had saved their
lives.

There were many hardships and tragedies. One
heartbreaking experience was to sell a bushel of wheat and
not get enough money te mail a letter to France.

They stayed on that homestead about three years and
unable to find a good supply of water left this good fertile
soil for sandy land southeast of Duck Leake near the South
Saskatchewan River across from the Batoche church. Here was
water, bush for firewood, open prairie for hay and some
buildings.

In summer the laundry was done at the river and clothes
spread on rocks on the shore to dry. One particular time
the river rose so swiftly that when Andréanne returned to
gather her clothes all had disappeared with the flow. After
that more so than ever she and the girls picked berries to
sell to buy material to replaca the lost garmente.

One especially long cold winter they ran out of hay.
Frangois knew there was some for sale ten miles west so
early one morning with the horse end an ox hitched to the
stick rack, he and his son Ferdinand left for a load.

Before they got there a raging blizzard came up and they had
to accept the hospitality of a neighbor friend, Mr. Mievre.
Finally three days later they had their load but had trouble
getting home because of the deep snow and the uneven pull of
their mismatched team. At times they even had to shovel to
clear the snowbound trail. Meanwhile at home to keep the
cow alive Andréanne had to use the hay from the boys!
mattress. The cow was well fed but the boys had to contend
themselves with a hard bed.

52

They stayed four years on that place. When wheat
started to sell at a better price he bought land south
between Duck Lake and La Plaine at $3.10 por acre. It had a
house and land ready to seed. Gradually he prospered but
Such progress was too slow for Frangois! ambitions. In 1906
he sold all he owned and was determined to move his family
to Algeria where as a young man he had been posted in the
French Army. There he had known wealthy French landowners,
He rented a house in town for his wife and nine children
where they wore to stay till he wrote them to follow.

He found Algeria was no longer the peaceful country he
had knovn. Feople carried guns for safety and their crops
were being robbed at night. On his way back he visited
France and realized that Canada was still the better place.

His return happened at a most opportune time as Mr.
Gourant was returning to France and had his property for
sale. ‘This land was of the best soil, seven miles south of
Duck Lake. With one stroke Francois had a farm and a
furnished home.

Frangois was a surveyor for the roads in the area and
he relied on René, Joe and Marguerite for the field work.
in 1911 Frangois became chairman of the La Plaine School
Board.

By 1916 he was 2 well established farmer. He owned a
new Model T Ford, a Fordson tractor, a fine herd of cattle
and good horses. He was chauffeured by one of his sons or a
daughter. He had at last reached his goal. In 1919 he and
his wife went for a whole year's holiday in France. In 1920
they retired in Duck Lake.

Lanovaz Farm Home Still Existing June 1995

Frangoise was just nine years old when she came to Canada with
her parents; 311 of her formal education was done in France.
Although she understood English quite well she was very selz-
conscious of her accent and spoke French almost exclusively.

Before her marriage she worked for Mrs. Mitchell and at the
Duck Lake Hotel as a waitress.

She met Alexis Périllat who had taken a homestead southeast of
Duck Lake and they were married in January 1905.

When Francoise and Alexis were first married they lived in town
and in the spring went to live in Lumsden where Alexis was employed
on the railroad. In December of 1906 they purchased a farm which
had a comfortable house and a barn. A good well was also an asset,
Alexis was a good farmer and a good manager. By 1918 he had

87

purchased a house in town to
enable the children to go to
school. Francoise, being very
busy with cooking, washing,
mending and household chores on
the farm, a housekeeper, Mrs.
Marie Rose Ouellette, was hired
to look after the children in
town. Most weekends they came
home for milk, eggs, meat and
other necessities from the farm.
When they were older, the
children drove a team of mules to
school and the house in town was
rented out.

In 1926 a large house with a
full basement was built on the
farm. Alexis enjoyed auction
sales, visiting and playing cards
with family and friends. After a
Severe stroke in 1929 left him

partially paralyzed, many of the
ferm decisions and physical work
were left to Francoise and the boys.
With the help from Marguerite
who was teaching school they
managed through the depression.
Alexis passed away in 1931.

In 1940 Francoise moved to
town to the house purchased in 1918.
She was very lonely at first until
some friends from La Plaine retired.
Frangoise always had a lovely
garden and many flowers. she
knitted bedspreads and tablecloths
and many pairs of mitts.

In the 50's' a friend, Mrs.
Anna Perrin, came to live with
Frangoise for a few years. Then
in the late 60's she went to live
with Marguerite in Winnipeg, always
returning for the summer holidays.
In 1974 she and Marguerite returned
heme for good. Her health was
failing by then. She passed away
in 1978.

Francoise, Isabelle, Marguerite, Lucien,
Pierre, Alexis Périllat, Edouard 1919

Marguerite Périllat
1909 - 1989

Marguerite started her teaching career at the La Plaine School
$56 continued teaching for many years in Saskatchewan and Manitoba
Bfte= retiring in Duck Lake in 1974 she became a good gardener: she
Zeved growing flowers inside and out. She spent many hours working
ae her yard

\ Marguerite loved to work and was never afraid to try new
=scires. Sho made many pots of soup and often brought some to
$aetiy and friends who were ill

She devoted her life to the education of children, even after
SStizing. She was always ready to give a helping hand She spent
Several years looking after her mother and went to the Rosthern

| Sospital every day of the last four months of her mother's life

She had a special love for animals, especially her dog Lady and
Ger cat Mimi. She and Lady enjoyed many long walks together

I, Solange, attended school in La Plaine. We were married in
April of 1942 and moved to a farm in 1944. We had nine children of
which seven are still living.

Gardening, milking cows, and baking was full time work. Due to
Zucien's ill health, the farm was sold in 1973, and we moved into
the town of Rosthern. Lucien passed away in May of 1976.

I began working at the Golden Sheaf Restaurant. I also was on
the Valley Action Board for a time. Duties at church were much in
my interest until January 1992 when health demanded that I slow
down, with a move to smaller quarters.

As the children are spread out, I have had the privilege of

Because of my hearing loss, I attended many different schools
that were equipped with special programs to help me to further my
education.

At 16 years of age I was fortunate to be accepted on highway
#11 construction. The foreman took me under his wing, having had a
handicap of his own. From then on I was able to run different
machinery for highway construction, as well as driving big trucks.

While living in Saskatoon on a job I met my future wife, Joan,
who also is deaf and mute, but had graduated from the School for the
Deaf.

We were married in February 1976. Two years later we purchased
a home in Osler where we raised our five children. I also do some
welding taught by my father. The two older girls have graduated and
left home. Sherry and Shirley are in Grade 12. Dennis still excels
in hockey with many trophies te show off.

We have now sold our property in Osler and moved to Luseland,
Saskatchewan. I will be trucking for Ace Excavations.

12

I was raised on the farm and went to school in Rosthern. I went
£0 Crane Valley, Sask. to work on a grain farm and finished high
school there. In 1977 I attended tha Technical School in Moose Jaw,
taking a commercial cooking course

T met my future wife, Cindy Simpson, born and raised in Mcose
Saw. We moved to Lloydminster where there were job opportunities

The boom of the oil patch called and I went to work on the
@rilling rigs, and at times driving truck. In 1987 when the oil
patch slowed down, I took a course for an Emergency Medical
Technician, and vorked as an ambulance attendant until 1992.

Cindy and I have been blessed with three children. The
children are very active in sports and music. The boys keep me busy
gs chauffeur to the hockey rink and ball games for practice and
ganes
‘This past year I attended college. 1 am thinking of going into
Forestry For the present time I am back with the oil rigs

douard & Cecile, Yvonne Périllat

80

Edouard & Cecile were farmers in the Rosthern area where their
children were born

They moved to Duck Lake in 1944 where they were owners in
partnership of a goneral store. They sold their share to Ferdinand
Lanovaz and ran their own business of trucking, gravel and
escavation with their son, Robert. Robert died accidently in a car
crash in 1959. Ed and Cecile were in business until the seventies
They retired to Kelowna, B.C, where he died in 1982. Cecile moved
te Montreal to reside with her daughter Lynne. Cecile's hobby is
knitting and crocheting slippers, afghan, shawls and crafts for
seniors homes and the underprivileged.

She 1s loved by all around her for her originality and beauty
ef all she creates and produces with her own two little hands.

Pierre Périllat
1915-1992

Pierre and I farmed for thirty-nine years on the farm Alexis

had purchased in 1906

We had a good life there.

lote of hard work

but it was a good place to raise our family
During the summer Bob Klassen and Harvey Doucette often came to

spend time with ue
hand.

They were always willing to give a helping

86

In 1972 wa started travelling to Burope, all across Canada, the
North West Territories and United States

In 1978 we built our retirement home in Duck Lake where I still
reside. Pierre passed away in 1992 after a long illness
I enjoy life, travelling, camping, fishing, doing crafts and
card playing along with volunteer work. I have taken part in many
community projects. In 1992 I was truly honoured to receive the
lion's "Citizen of the Year" award,

The highlights for me are having the children, grandchildren
and great grandchildren get togethers. As of now we are a family of
thirty-nine.

Paul's first career choice was always farming. After working

in partnership with his father for a few years, Paul purchased land
ene mile north and east of the home farm and began establishing his
own mixed farming operation. All the buildings and miles of fence
were erected over a period of several years in the early seventies,
and we continue adding buildings and corrals as time goes on.

Paul has been active in sports including fastball, curling, old
time hockey and downhill skiing--interests which both sons have in
common. They also share Paul's love of agriculture and are
presently enrolled at the University of Saskatchewan in tha College
of Agriculture. Colin will receive his Bachelor of Arts in
Agriculture in May 1996, Brian has a emall herd of purebred
Limousin cattle and enjoys working with them at home, as well as
attending sales and shows.

T have not been employed as a veterinarian for many years but
help out at home. Paul and I are both involved in several community
organizations.

In 1962 I left Duck Lake for St, Hyacinthe, Quebec joining the
Order of the Sisters of Presentation of Mary. Three years later I
started my nurses training at St. Boniface General Hospital. It was
there in 1967 that I changed my religious to secular life.

My cousin, Lillian Périllat was working in Winnipeg and she
decided that I needed a social life. so I went on a blind date
to the Armed Forces Base thus meeting my future husband

Gerry Gore-Langton, born in Vancouver, lived in Victoria for
eighteen years and joined the Armed Forces in 1965

We were married in 1968 and within six months we were
transferred to Soest W. Germany. We made the most of our stay
there, travelling to many countries. We have two daughters, Chantal
and Corina, both born in Germany. It was interesting that we had tc
file citizenship papers for the girls when we returned to Canada

In 1973 we were transferred to Chilliwack. I returned to work
on a casual basis at first. When we bought our small farm in
Rosedale I left the farming to Gerry and went to work fulltime at
the Chilliwack General Hospital. Our life wae busy with family
work and pastimes such as camping and the 4H movement

We now live in Sardis. Gerry works at the Oil and Lube Bay for
the Esso Company and I'm still employed at the hospital

André, the youngest son of Pierre and Ida Périllat, graduated
from Stobart High School and then attended the University of
Saskatchewan. He returned home to work with his father and in 1974
started purchasing the family farm. He has been a successful mixed
farmer every since.

Since our marriage in 1984, André and I have been busy with the
farm but mostly raising our four children. We are also very active
in various community organizations such as the Duck Lake Lions Club,
Duck Lake Historical Museum, and the Duck Lake History Book
Committee for André and myself, the Duck Lake Murals Board, Duck
Lake French Cultural Association, Duck Lake French Preschool and the
Church Music Ministry. André and I also enjoy collecting antiques
and collectibles.

Life on the farm has its ups and downs but in our opinion there
is no greater place to live and raise a family.

i i i l Philippe Turbide

‘The youngest of a family of six, I was born in 1922 in a log
house some five miles southeast of Duck Lake. My earliest
recollections are of my mother making scap, scrubbing on her knees
the rough wooden floor, warning me when she was baking bread not to
jump near the stove, and of my sister Marguerite holding me by the
ear, a wash cloth in her hand, of my yelling in simulated pain and
of my mother scolding my sister for hurting me.

The first three years of my schooling was in Duck Lake during
which time due either to distance, bad weather or a chronic earache
I was not always present. For grades 4 to 6 I attended La Plaine
School where Marguerite was the teacher. We lived, except for the
weekends, in the rooms above the school

Thanks to my sister Marguerite I was at 14 sent to St. Peter's
College in Muenster and then to Normal School in Saskatoon

After one year of teaching in Millerdale School (near
Kerrobert) I decided to go to university, working during the summers
at whatever I could find--one summer on a farm stocking at $4.00 a
day, another summer on the railway near Maidstone and another summer
on the Alaska Highway as a kitchen help for the American army

On graduation from University of Saskatchewan I taught again
for one year in Fernie, B.C., and for one year in Mission, B.C. Tt
was there, towards the end of the school year that I decided to buy
@ car-load of third-rate lumber (the entire car-load including the
transportation cost me $700 00 which was half of my yearly pay as a
teacher), This lumber I unloaded in July on an empty lot where the
Present D & H Lumber Yard now stands (I had called it the Service
Lumber Yards) and immediately began to build a granary which before
it was completed I was able to sell for $100.00--the down payment
paid for the shingles to cover the roof. The cuttings were used to

106

build outdoor toilets for which there was a ready market and sold
for whatever the market could bear.

By August a small shed had been built to store the lumber and
by September the business, lock, stock and goodwill was sin The
profit made paid for two years of studies at McGill University in
Montréal.

In 1949, as a student
in Montréal I was required
to attend a very large
class. The professor in his
first lecture asked that
those students who might
have dirriculty seeing the
blackboard from where they
were seated raise their
hands. Since I have always
been short-sighted I did so.
He then proceeded to arrange
all who had raised their
hands to be seated in
alphabetical order. My name
beginning with a "P", as was
that of my subsequent wife,
We Were seated next to one
another,

We talked, we compared notes, ve shared books and at the end of
the year Suzanne left Montréal in order to continue her studies in
Europe. No problem, I said, I would, during the coming summer, earn
the Tequired money to join her. My business venture that summer was
2 dismal failure. I did not even have enough money to pay for the
fare on a cattle boat.

teen later 2 magazine with international coverage published
an artical

Suzanne & Arthur Périllat 1993

They were greatly admired. I was asked to accompany her and
her uncle to a picture framing shop. After a fey complimentary

week's Salary for most people and in the West lumber wa thet
selling for $60.00 a thousand board feet. wath my knowledge of the
prace Of lumber I quickly realized that a profit could be made by
framing pictures.

Forty-five years later, suzanne and I (and now with our
daughter Frangoise) are still actively engaged in the selling and
framing of pictures

Ferdinand was seven years old when, in 1894, he and his parents
and four siblings came to Canada. They settled on a homestead in
the Carlton area about fourteen miles west of Duck lake.

Times were hard and soon Ferdinand had to find work. In 1900
when only 14, he worked for two summers at Jackfish Lake, at first
for $8.00 a month, then for $15.00 a month.

The summer of 1904 he worked on the C.N. Railway for 14 1/2¢ an
hour for ten hours a day. then for several years he worked on farms
and on road construction in the local area.

Ferdinand's schooling consisted of 1 1/2 years in France and
one winter in Duck Lake, but he did not let this deter him. With
time reading became his favourite pastime.

In 1910 he rented a quarter of land from his future uncle,
Joseph Forestier seven miles south of Duck Lake. Then in 1914 he
bought this land For $5,250.00. Over the years he bought more land

116

until he owned six quarters. The place is now farmed by his
grandson, Albert Lanovaz.

In 1911 he married a neighbour's daughter, Ernestine Bouvard.
The Bouvards had come to Canada May 5, 1896. At 16 Ernestine had
been employed by Duck Lake merchants, Mr. 6 Mrs. Amiot. She worked
three years, earning $7.00 a month keeping house and learning how to
sev. Mrs- Aniot was an experienced seamstress. Then for tvo years
she was clerk in their store where she earned $15.00 a month and
living with them all that time.

Ferdinand and Ernestine were married in the present Blessed
Sacrament Church by Father Tharsis Schmit. their wedding
transportation was a "fiacre" drawn by two horses driven by Gabriol
Laplante. René Lanovaz was their bestman and Emilie Bouvard wae
bridesmaid,

Tae

Ferdinand & Ernestine omyarely aniesae (11

In 1918 Ferdinand bought his first car, a four-door Model 7
Ford touring car which he drove for 13 years.

In the late '20's he and his brother Joe bought a steam
threshing outfit. They did custom work for relatives and
neighbours. It was quite an event when a crew of 24 men converged
on @ farm, The lady of the house had some hustling to do to feed
ail these hungry men. In time they purchased a caboose and hired a
cook to follow the outfit.

There was a tractor on the farm for making chop and cutfeed,
but it was 1937 before @ John Deere tractor was bought for field
work.

Ferdinand was noted as an outstanding farmer and excelled as a
cattle man. For twenty years he was involved in school affairs. He
worked tirelessly in the promotion of L'ACFC for the survival of the
French language and culture. He was an active original member of
the Saskatchewan Wheat Fool, He enjoyed sports, still curling in
his late 70's and an avid and sharp cribbage player even at the age
of 97.

He and Ernestine retired from the farm in 1948. For several
years he was partner in the Lanovaz & Périllat store, and later the
Lanovaz & Farwell store (formerly the Pozer store).

After his retirement he visited his native France three times,
the last time when he was nearly 90.

On November 5, 1979, due to failing health caused by
Parkinson's Disease he moved into Mont St. Joseph Nursing Home in
Prince Albert. He lived there until his death at the age of 97
years and 10 months. Ernestine died in 1974 from intestinal cancer
at the age of 85.

Ferd Lanovaz’s Farm in the early 40's
‘Two story granary in background was the site of the Bouvard ferm.
The big red barn was an Eaton package built by Jos Perret.

Ferdinand retired in the same house in 1948

Marie (Pogu) and Ernest Lanovaz were married March 8, 1943 in a
double ceremony with brother and sister Lucie (Lanovaz) and Auguste
Pogu. The only way to travel in those days was with horses and

caboose.

The couple made their home on
in the Rosthern municipality.
they farmed together.

a farm in the La Plaine district,

They vere close to his dad's home and

te Plaine was a very friendly place. In winter when chores
were done a lot of visiting and card playing with neighbors took
place. In summer all were busy with field work with horses, cattle,
pigs and chickens to look after, The women had large gardens to
look after also.

There was no electricity and no water taps. The homes were
heated with wood so there was wood cutting and splitting to be done.
They were blessed with six children. Annette, being the
oldest, was boarded out in Duck Lake to attend school for one year
then Paul joined her there for another two years. When Guy started

school they went to the La Plaine School with a horse and sled in

Paul worked as a surveyor, Guy became a farmer, Claire went cs
ceacher's college, Benoit joined the R.C.M.P and Claudatte worked

in the fall of 1969 Ernest and Marie sold the farm and retired
re uGk Jake to a new home they had built They bought the pool
oom and operated it until 1973

Before her marriage Marie had boon a seamstress since finishing
school, making a living for her mother and two younger sisters; ne
when she retired to town and had time to spare, she again took an

She took care of her aging mother who lived next door until she

Marie still lives in her home. She is not sewing any more--65
years of it was enough. She keeps busy in her garden, visits the
residents in the nursing home and belongs to the Senior's Club.

She makes a home for Guy and Paul who are bachelors. Paul
lives in his own home but boards at his mother's.

After Julien and I were married in November 1968 we settled
down on the farm 3.5 miles north of Duck Lake. Julien had been
farming there since 1957 and I had been working in Saskatoon since
graduation. Having been raised on a farm six miles northeast of
Rosthern I was quite familiar with life on the farm but had
forgotten just how hectic it could be.

In 1985 we decided we needed a change and moved to Salmon Arm,
B.C. We were all looking forward to a different lifestyle with all
its new challenges; but the biggest of all was leaving family and
friends behind. For the next few years Julien would be spending
most of the time back on the farm. Once we ranted out the farm he
was able to spend more time at home and eventually started driving
school bus.

I have been doing bookkeeping for a forestry consulting firm
for the past eight years.

Luc has been involved in the ski and golf industry for six
years now. He works out at Big White near Kelowna for the winters
and has spent a couple of summers working at a ski resort in
Australia.

Monique is expecting to complete her teaching degree in a
couple of years at UBC.

And as soon as Joel graduated he headed back to the prairies
where he has been working ever since.

I graduated from high school in 1967, the year of Canada's
100th birthday. I attended Teacher's College for one year. I
taught on Beardy's Reserve the following year then returned to
university for another year to get my Standard A Certificate

On August 1,1970 I married Ronald Michael Bazylak from Duck
Lake. Our first two years of marriage were spent in Winnipag,
Manitoba where Ron worked in the accounting department for a truck
company, International Harvester. Then Ren was transferred to
Regina, Sack. where he continued te work for the next year and a
half. I taught in both cities during this period.

In December of 1973 we moved to Duck Lake where Ron was to
realize his ambition of owning his own business. We purchased a
grocery and meat store from Leonard Doucette. The store is now
called R.B. Foods. I started teaching shortiy into the new year,
finished that year and continued for another year before we started
our family.

These years and the next few kept us very busy, building up a
business, raising a family, being involved in community affairs,
moving several times, building a home, and driving kids to various
activities. I worked on the Library Board and with the Scout-Guide
movement. When the kids were back in school I returned to teach
part-time, completed my Education Degree during this time then went
on to teach full time. I like to read, walk, cross-country ski,
downhill ski and hike in the woods. Ron was on several
organizations and committees to promote growth and tourism in the

130

community. He committed much time to establishing and building a
medical clinic, a nursing home and a new interpretive center to
xeplace the museum. In 1990 Ron received the Citizen of the Year
Award for his hardworking efforts in the community. Ron enjoys
fishing, skiing, and watching movies to relax.

The kids enjoyed being read to, playing board games and card
games, cross-country and downhill skiing, and jumping on the
trampoline. Annual ski trips with family friends were a highlight
for everyone. The boys went on several fishing trips with their
father when they got older. We went on one family fishing trip with
friends to Jim's Camp by Nistowiak Falls near Stanley Mission.
During the summer of 199) the kids and I went on a camping trip with
my sister Claudette and her family and our nephew, Joel Pelletier
We went to visit my brother Ben and his family in Montreal, Quebec
As they got older the kids all took turns working at the store on
weekends or during the summer holidays

Ron & Claire Bazylak Family 1993

Patricia, Lee, Ryan Bazylak 1982

Growing up, Patricia loved girl guides, summer camps, skiing,
skating, swimming and horseback riding lessons. When she was six,
she went on her first major trip to Montreal with her mom. She
played the flute in the school band, and took piano lessons
throughout her school years. She went to Rivier Academy in Prince
Albert for her Grades 10 and 11 where she got into running, playing
basketball, started canceing and playing the guitar. The summer
after Grade 11 Tricia went skydiving with her cousin Monique
Pelletier. She also attended an International camp at Lester B.
Pearson College in B.C. where she met kids from around the world and
was introduced to rock climbing. She finished her high school with
honors at Holy Cross in Saskatoon and then took a year of university
am Arts and Sciences. Tricia lived with Ron's sister Lorraine
during this time and was joined by her brother, Ryan, in the last
year she was in Saskatoon, Together they took karate training at
the U of S$. The following summer Tricia trained and worked for a
canoe guiding company in Jasper, Alberta and in the Nahanni National
Park, N.W.T. It was a great experience for her. That winter, she
worked as a ski instructor in Big White, Kelowna, B.C. where her
cousin, Luc Pelletier, also worked. She loved teaching, especially
the little kids, and soon got to be known as Trish the Fish. She
received the rookie of the year award that season, not to be outdone
by Luc, who received the instructor of the year award. In the
spring of 1995 Tricia took an industrial first aid attendant course
before roturning to the Northwest Territories for another year of
canoe guiding and rafting trips. Presently Tricia is working
another winter on the ski hill. In the spring she will be taking an
E.M.T. course for emexgency medical technicians at SIAST in
Saskatoon. In the foreseeable future Patricia expects to do some
more paddling end rock climbing, go back to university and continue
ski instructing.

A Fishing Trip 1991
Ryan, Ron, Lee Bazylak

132

When Ryan was little he enjoyed reading, puzzles of all kinds,
end building anything and everything with lego. He belonged to
Beavers and Boy Scouts, played hockey and baseball, took swimning
lessons, and went to summer camps. Later he took up skiing and
martial arts. He loved to draw and paint. He played the saxophone
in the schcol band and took piano lessons for most of his school
years, In the springtime he liked to help coach the little league
players who were just learning to play baseball. Summers were
filled with camps, canoe trips, and then working at youth camps when
he got older. He especially loved to go fishing with his father.
Following this, is an essay Ryan wrote on his first fishing
experience. In the summer of 1993 when he was 15 Ryan went on a
memorable bus tour to Denver, Colorado to see the Pope. For hie
Grade 10 and 11 Ryan attended Holy Cross High School in Saskatoon.
During those two years he joined the school wrestling team and tock
karate training with his sister, Tricia. He even got a flying
jesson in a small aerobatic aircraft, in a special program offered
through the school. This year Ryan applied for, and received a full
two year scholarship to attend Lester B. Pearson College of the
Pacific, an International school, in Victoria, B.C. He is very
excited about going there and having the opportunity to live and
work with students from around the world. In the first five months
he has been there Ryan has found the International Baccalaureate
program much more demanding than he had anticipated. Juggling and
balancing studies, college activities and socializing is a major
Challenge. Some extra curricular activities and service projects he
has chosen are coastwatch (kayaking, ocean vehicle and radio
operator), rock climbing, jazz dance, choir, drama, environmental
Program and working with the physically challenged.

FISHING by Ryan Bazylak

I xemenber my first fishing experience. My dad took my brother
hee, my cousin Jarvis, my uncle Wayne, and I down to the river by
the Batoche ferry. Dad had a rod and the rest of us had set up
Stationary lines. Well, my dad set them up. I remember watching
that line intently, just waiting for the slightest jiggle. I kept
yelling "Dad, dad! I got one!" And he would tell me that it was
just the hooks in the current. "You'll know when you get a bite."
Then my uncle would say "Scooter (Jarvis) will know. He's done
this." But what did he know. He wasn't any older than I was. I
caught the first fish that day. "A good sized pickerel," Dad said.
£ T remember correctly I think it was the only fish caught that
sunny Sunday at the river. I never did oat that fish. t's still
probably downstairs in the freezer. That Sunday was one of the best
days of my life.

133

Lee was born on May 16, 1979. He started out the first two
years mostly crying and screaming. We finally found out he had an
allergy to food coloring. Within a couple of days he was a changed
baby, and life for us became normal again, We had to be|really
careful about the food he ate. Patricia, who often watched over him
when all three went out to play, would warn anyone who tried to feed
him candy that they would have to look after him. Although he

eventually outgrew this, he still has allergy problems in the spring
and fall. In his early years Lee enjoyed Beavers, hockey and
baseball. He loved to tinker with anything and everything, use his
amagination and "think" hours away. He attended a few summer Bible
camps located south of Kindersley with Tricia, Ryan and cousin
Jarvis. He also took piano lessons, but soon switched to the
trumpet when he could take band at school. Lee has enjoyed downhill
and cross-country skiing in the winter since he was small. For the
past three summers Lee has been attending St. John Bosco Camp in the
Narrow Hills Provincial Park where he enjoys hiking, biking and
canoeing. After this year's six waek training course he hopes to
work for the next few years as a staff mombor. Lee hopes to
complete his grade 12 this year and move on to broader avenues. He
likes to spend most of his time with friends, working at his job at
the Family Center and doing schoolwork. . . in that order. Although
he is unsure of his future plans at the moment, he'll figure them
out sooner or later. He's always had a passion for trains, so who
knows. . .(Qx I could bum off my parents till i'm 35

BR. Jean Pogu, Muriel Raabe, Auguste Pogu
Auguste & Lucie Pogu 1943 F.R. Cécile Buehler, Lucie Pogu, Diane Roden

140

I was born and grew up on the farm in the La Plaine School
District and attended that school until grade 8 then stayed at my
grandparents and aunt in Duck Lake for High School. Because of my
mother's savere and frequent migraines I never worked out but helped
at home till I married in 1943.

Life on the farm wes not alvays only work. In winter our good
neighbors Mr. Emile Perret and later Uncle Réne Lanovaz had a
skating rink. We owe them much gratitude for understanding the need
of entertainment for the young people and giving so generously of
their time to provide this winter sport.

In summer there was Sunday afternoon gatherings of young and
old at the school to participate in ball games, root for their
favorite teams or just visit with neighbors. There were the
occasional school dances or house parties. Those too young to join
in found it fun just to watch.

Getting married locally to a farmer my life style did not
change much--same work, same town, same church. The mode of travel
for a time was different in summer as we did not have a car till a
couple of years later but we were less than three miles from town.
In winter those years roads were not kept open for cars once the
snowdrifts blocked the roads so everyone travelled with horse and
sled.

Diane, Muriel & Cecile Pogu

149

At Dad's we'd had a car since 1918 so summer travel was no
problem. Winter however the one hour trip to town or church was
something else. On very cold days a foot warmer or hot bricks vas
almost a necessity as were several layers of carriage robes.

With time Dad's old Ford touring lost its top and was not too
elegant a conveyance but we were fortunate it never did have to be
converted into a Bennett wagon as were the horse-drawn car chassis
called.

Auguste and I had a humble beginning with a few milk cows, a
couple of pigs and four horses. Gradually the virgin land was put
under cultivation, more land was bought and the cattle herd
increased. For many years we had live -in hired help. later the
farm was mechanized and thus work made easier. For years we drove
the children to school, until a bus route was established. That
trip also served to bring the cans of cream to the station to be
shipped by train.

People in my generation saw incredible changes in life style.
At Dad's I don't remember not having had a phone but here it wae
1955 before the line was brought in. Electric power lines also came
here in 1955, however Auguste being very resourceful we did have a
power plant of our own but these did not carry appliances such as
stoves, fridges, freezers, etc.

It must have been in the mid 30's before we had a radio at
Dad's and these were battery run and had to be used sparingly. TV's
came in 1954 but we did not own one till about 1960. Without all
the automated facilities, spare moments were not that plentiful and
we did not miss that kind of entertainment.

Auguste has always had an interest in community affairs and has
served on a number of committees such as school, church, museum,
wheat pool, etc. We both enjoy the quiet atmosphere of country
living. We love gardening. Auguste likes tinkering in the shop. I
like to do crochet work but best of all comes reading. We both
appreciate our local library. We are retired on the farm but when
work is pressing we're ready to give 2 helping hand. We enjoy the
occasional trip.

My sister Marie and I travelled to France with our Dad in 1975.
He introduced us to our many relatives. Auguste, his sisters marie
and Helene and I returned in 1985 and toured more of the country.

We take a break from the Saskatchewan winters to visit our daughter
Muriel and family in California.

In 1993 we celebrated our 50th wedding anniversary with all the
family attending. We also had Mr. & Mrs. Airiau from France,
relatives of the Pogus who arranged their trip to Canada to be
present at our celebration.

142

Auguste Pogu, lan Buehler

What memories I have of growing up on the farm (mostly fond,
but a few not so!)--gardening, collecting eggs, wood, and snow (to
melted in a drum for laundry and cleaning), cooking, baking, and
ing, picking wild berries, cleaning, feeding the animals, and my
¢ "unfaverite chore", milking cows.
Until I was twelve we lived in the log house built by the Pogu
eat grandparents which consisted of a large country kitchen,
‘ving room and dining room, one bedroom shared by mom and dad and
, an attic for storage and sleeping area for the hired men, and
n unfinished basement. Cecile had a crib in the dining room, while
Diane and I slept on the couch in the living room. Two fabric-
red apple crates made up our dresser. Monday was laundry day
the gas-powered washing machine was moved next to the kitchen
tove. Saturday wes cleaning and baking day. sunday was the day of
(except for milking cows!!). I look back with amusement at the
saturday night bath ritual. The galvanized tub, which had been
auled into the middle of the kitchen next to the wood stove, was
:lled with hot water that had been heated in a large boiler. All
the kids took turns jumping in and scrubbing. When I was twelve
bathroom and two bedrooms ware added to the house. Note--a
tthroem! We did not have one until then. A wash basin in the
tchen, a pail in the dark dungeon-like basement to be used only at
Right and during the cold of winter, and an outhouse by the garden
mas the extent of our bathroom. The new bathroom was a major
provement, but a few years elapsed before a flush toilet and
‘thtub were installed. The Saturday-only bath continued, except

now we had privacy!
our own bedroom.

How thrilled Diane, Cecile and I were to have

144

After church each Sunday we met with the extended family at
Grandpére and Grandmare Lanovaz's house across from the church and
Grandmére Pogu's just a block away. The men had their "p'tit coup
de piquette"; the women enjoyed their tea or coffee; and there was
punch, cookies, and candies for the kids, and perhaps Tante Lydie's
"menthes". Christmas and all church holidays were a time of family
celebrations. We looked forward to the May Recital and Christmas
concert at the convent, the Fall Fowl Supper and Bazaar at the
church hall, the Pilgrimage at St. Lawrence on July 16th, the Sports
Day in July and all the summer family picnics. indlese hours in
November and December were spent practicing for Midnight Mass.

Andrea & Melanie Raabe

Muriel Pogu, Hostess, 1967 Expo in Montreal

145

Because of my father's very ingenious and mechanical mind, we
were the only farm family for miles with our own generator. I do
not remember using gas lamps and candles, but vividly remember
visiting neighbors and relatives who did. I do remember using the
cast iron and not having a fridge until my teens. The telephone and
T.V. came only when I was in high school.

Some big treats were an occasional movie in town, "Cing sous"
from Grandpére Lanovaz for an ice cream cone if we per chance ran
into him in front of the Lanovaz store, a day at the fair in P.A.
and perhaps a yearly trip to Saskatoon. As tecnagers, we went
skating, curling, te house parties, hockey games, dances, or
cruising around town. It was @ thrill when Aunt Yvonne would visit,
bringing us her store-bought hand-me-downs. Except for a few items
from Sears or Eaton's catalog, our clothes were home-made, I'm not
complaining as mom was a wonderful seamstress; but it sure felt
great to wear bought things once in a while.

‘A few unfavorable memories to be added to milking cows were the
unbearable bouts with poison ivy, which once sent me to the
hospital, and the numerous hours spent with hot mustard plasters on
my chest (which surprisingly did not leave third degree burn scars!)
in an attempt to rid me of bronchitis.

During the vinters of my first two years of school I boarded at
the convent, at the Maier's and at Grandpa and Grandma's in town.

At Stobart School, academics were stressed, and we did hours of
homework and studying. There was no such thing as P.E. or Drama,
but we did have Art and Glee Club. The nuns were so strict--no
horsing around in their presence! Although the school was co-ed the
boys entered and exited through one door and the girls through
another to separate playgrounds.

After completing a business course at L'Institue des Filles de
la Province in P.A. I worked at the University Hospital in
Saskatoon. Six months of 1965 were spent touring Europe; part of
that time working in London and Germany. While working in a
bookbindery in Ulm, Germany I met an American soldier, the man who
little did I know at the time would become my husband. Meeting
Russ, the distant relatives in La Haute Savoie and visiting the
actual places where my grandparents had lived were highlights of the

trip. Upon returning to Saskatoon I went back to work at the
University Hospital.

What a feather in my cap it was when I was one of three chosen
to represent Sask. as a Hostess at Expo 67 in Montreal! Following
six months of intense training, I gave tours, and manned Information
Booths. I felt honored to stand on the Tribune of Honor with Nelson
A. Rockefeller on New York State Day and Haile Sellasie on Ethiopia
Day, as well as at the closing ceremony of Expo. After a month's
relaxing trip in the West Indies, I returned to Saskatoon to attend
Teacher's College. My teaching career at St. Thomas in Saskatoon
was short-lived, as after only two years I moved to California.

146

Russ was born in Hollywood, California and had spent most of
his life in Southern California, with the exception of a few years
in Montana as a child, a year with a family in Austria and three
years in the U.S. Army in Germany as a young adult. Wis father was
of German/Dutch descent from Ohio and his mother from Detroit was of
Irish/English descent. He has two sisters, Denise in Las Vegas and
Kathy in Huntington Beach, California. He went to school with Steve
Martin, Every family has a few skeletons in the closet, right!!
Russ‘ maternal grandmother, Ethel Parker from Kentucky, was a cousin
of Butch Cassidy (from Butch Cassidy and the Sundance Kid) and
Bonnie Parker (of the notorious Bonnie & Clyde duo). Believe me,
Russ did not inherit their shady characteristics. Russ has keen
involved in transportation for many years. He is presently employed
with Kindersley Transport Ltd. out of Saskatoon as one of their
Southern California representatives.

Our first home was a small, but cozy apartment on the beach in
the Belmont Shore area of Long Beach and I worked for an Orthopedic
Clinic. We bought our first house in Anaheim in 1973 and there I
started my first business, processing medical insurance forms.
Andrea, who has become a lovely young lady, was born at St. Joseph
Hospitel in Orange in 1974. Sister Therese Cecillon, a nursing nun
who had grown up near Duck Lake was at my side. In 1975 we moved to
Thousand Oaks and have lived in our home in the Wildwood area since.
Our blond-haired, blue-eyed Melanie was born here at Los Robles
Hospital. For a few years I had a Shaklee business, followed by
almost ten years of bookkeeping, bidding and estimating for a
Construction company. In the last fow years I have vorked for the
school district while taking an advanced medical transcription
course. TI now run an after-school program at a school nearby, with
Plans to start a medical transcription business

We enjoy travelling and camping. ‘he trips back to
Saskatchewan which have not been as frequent as I would have liked
them to be, have been the most memorable and enjoyable. The older I
get the more I appreciate my family. I'll take the fine upbringing
I received and the love and security I felt any day over the
materialism.

After almost twenty-five years living in the U.S. I should
consider California home, which in a way I do (if only there were no
earthquakes), but I will never forget my roots and will never

renounce my Canadian citizenship. As my dad once said, "You can
take the girl out of the country, but you can't take the country out
of the girl."

I am so proud and feel blessed to be a Lanovaz descendent!

147

I have always had more energy than I knew what to do with. The
first thing I did at 16 months old when we moved here te 7,0, was to
check out the fireplace. I don't remember, of course, but my mom
says I was having a ball and was covered from head to toe with soot.
The water had not yet been turned on in the house!

Our new swimming pool was ready in time for my sixth birthday
party. Between gymnastics and swimming I didn't have time for much
else. In high school I was on the Swimming Team and then the Diving
Team. My gymnastics background prepared me well to be a diver, and
in my Junior year I qualified for CIF which is the Southern
California high school finals. I graduated from the Thousand Oaks
High School in 1993. While a Senior I worked part time 2s a child
care assistant at Amgen, a large biotech company nearby. I am now a
childcare leader there and attend Moorpark Junior College near hone
at night. Although I love working with children, I would eventually
like a career with animals, possibly as an animal trainer.

I've enjoyed our family trips especially the trips to the farm
in Canada. Several years ago when camping in a trailer in the
Sierras, 2 big bear and her cub broke our station wagon's window
looking for food. It was rather crowded in the trailer and I had
considered sleeping in the beck of the station wagon that night but
had chickened out!! ‘Two years ago I went to Hawaii and just
recently toured the San Francisco area.

148

I busy myself with work, school, the gym and week-end trips to
Orange County. I try to give a little time to my snake Indy
(Indiana Jones) which I got as a gift four years ago--much to my
mom's diemay! A few years ago he got loose for the whole winter.
My dad found him in the cutlery drawer in the spring. I guess he
had hibernated in the kitchen cupboards!

Andrea Raabe & her pet snake

I guess you could say I ama typical California teenage girl.
I love hot weather and going to the beach. Lately I hava been
learning to surf. I'ma Junior at Thousand Oaks High School and
very much involved in diving, dance and art. This is my third
season as a varsity diver and last year I made CIF finals. I'm
shooting for it again this year. I lock forward to dance class
every day and we are practicing hard for the big dance concert
coming up in the spring. When my mom finds time she gives me pisno
lessons. Art is the love of my life. I particularly love doing
still life and portraits. My art teacher has already enrolled me in
his AP (Advanced Placement) Art Class next year which will give me
college credits. A career in Art is what I would like,

I've enjoyed all our family trips, but I've particularly loved
the ones to the farm in Canada. Roaming the countryside on the Quad
with my cousins has been a blast. When I was four years old I
learned to ride my cousin, Ian's bike in the shop. One time grandma
rigged up magnets on the end of long wires and she paid my sister
and me a penny for each nail we picked up from the ground around the
farm. Ever since I can remember, we have gone to Las Vegas at least
three or four times a year to visit my Aunt Denise, Uncle Ed, and
cousins Jennifer and Todd. Jennifer and I spend all our school
breaks and part of summer vacation together. Last year she came to
Canada with us

150

I look forward to grandpa and grandma's visit every winter, and
hopefully next year we will all be able to go back to Canada for
part of the sumer.

After completing Grade 12 in 1963 from Stobart High School, T
enrolled in the two-year Registered Radiological Technology program
at University Hospital in Saskatoon. After graduation I accepted a
Position at the Langenburg Union Hospital in 1965, with the
intention of working one year and then returning to university. 1
stayed for thirty years. In 1966 I married Rod Reden, who was
employed at the IMC Potash Mine. The following year our son, Marc
James was born. The year after we were blessed with Jo-Anne Louise.

I worked part-time at the hospital as an x-ray technician and
in the admissions office until 1978. In 1973 we purchased the

ternational Harvester farm equipment dealership in Langenburg, and
ren the operation for the next eleven years before selling it and
moving on to full-time farming. Marc joined us in the farm
operations in 1986. We enjoyed a family trip to Australia to visit
Rod's brother in 1982. Over the years we enjoyed travelling to many
Parts of the world, with a special interest in South America.

In 1994 Rod and I separated and I moved to Calgary. I am
currently employed as a business manager for a veterinary hospital
with a satellite clinic.

I was born in the middle of haying, and one week after the St.
Laurent Pilgrimage. I'm certain my arrival was quite a disruption
but with the able assistance of Marie Ange Pelletier in the house,
the farm kept on humming.

Memories of my early years are quite vague. Life revolved
around the farm, my big sisters, and going to town to visit cousins
or to shop at Lanovaz & Farwell. If we were lucky Grand-pére
Lanovaz would be sitting outside the store and he always had "cing
sous" for us to buy an ice cream cone. Sunday always seemed to
include going to Grand-pére's after church. There we could look
forward to Grand-mére's cookies and Tante Alidie had peppermints to
pass around to the children. The men had "un petit coup" as they
discussed the state of affairs. At Grand-pére's we spoke French,
“or else". I'm not sure what the "or else” was but Tante Marie
(Ernest) Lanovaz sticks in my memory as the one who used to come to
us and say, "Parlez Francais, Grand-pére . . ".

School was great. I loved the socializing opportunities that
cama with it. By the time T was in Grade 4 or 5 I could often be
found in the primary classes helping the teachers mark their
workbooks. I thought it vas quite a privilege. Today marking is
the task I like the least about my job.

Post high school saw me off to Saskatoon Business College and
from there to Prince Albert working as a bilingual secretary. 1
soon realized that I wasn't cut out for sitting at a desk. I needed

156

to be able te move more than my fingers. In January 1971 I entered
the College of Education at the University of Regina. I majored in
Business Education and also took a few French classes because they’d
be easy.

Cécile & Ivan Buehler
Tan Buehler 1 1/2 yeras old
Yould that foretell a career wielding a hammer?’

Ian, David & Lise Buehler

187

At the U of RI met my huskand to be, a sort of hippie, Ivan
Buehler, also a student of Business Education, In the fall of 1972
Ivan started teaching at Prince Albert Technical High. We were
married in October of that year and I joined the staff at the school
in January 1973.

In 1976 we decided it was time to move on. Harris,
Saskatchewan neaded two teachers, one for business education and one
for French. And so began my career as a French teacher. As we
started our family in 1979 we began to look for positions that would
offer more job security. Humboldt became our next destination.

In July after Ian was born we moved to Humboldt. I was
perfectly happy to be at home with our new baby but as he approached
his first birthday, I was offered a half-time position teaching
French at Humboldt Public School. And so again the times when we
were forced to "parlez Frangais" was going to pay off. The last two
years T've also been teaching at Humboldt Collegiate to add another
rewarding challenge to my day.

Last summer I realized a lifelong dream. Ivan, our three
children and I spent the summer holidays in Europe. Tt was a summer
of finding our roots: Ivan's in southern England, the Pogu's and
Fiolleau's in Vendée, and the Lanovaz and Bouvard's in Savoie.

Grand-pare Lanovaz had always spoken with passion of his
beloved Savoie. It was very emotional to visit the places we'd
heard of so often. Our children found La Bénite Fontaine, a small
church and grotto near La Roche sur Foron to be the best church they
visited all summer. I attended Sunday mass at Arenthon, the church
where the Lanovaz children would have been baptized. I saw the
Lanovaz house and met a gentleman, living across the road, who
remembered Tante Frangoise Périllat and Grand-pére visiting. He
felt that Grand-pére yearned to stey in France but his family was in
Canada. We visited the remains of the "moulin Bouvard" and the
house where my great great grandfather Bouvard died.

our children continue to be the centre of our universe.

Besides family travel, we enjoy family time at the Buehler cottage
at Moosomin Lake and at the Pogu farm in Duck Lake.

The day after his parents finished teaching in Harris, Tan was
bern in St. Paul's Hospital, Saskatoon. He is now in grade 11 and
participates in volleyball, basketball, rollerblading, snowboarding,
waterskiing, and coaches gymnastics. He is interested in music,
reading, philosophy, and his social life (not necessarily in that
order) .

David, the family philosopher, was born in University Hospital,
Saskatoon. He is now in grade 7 and participates in soccer,
baseball, volleyball, basketball, rollerblading and gymnastics. He
has a strong interest in music, reading and television.

Lise, the youngest of the family, was born in St. Elizabeth
Hospital, Humboldt. She is now in grade 5 and participates in
dance, soccer, softball, rollerblading, and gymnastics. She also
takes piano lessons in her free time and likes to do crafts.

158

Jean arrived in 1956, a long awaited boy in the Pogu family.
He tock elementary and high school at Stobart and his education did
not stop there. His inquisitive nature has him constantly studying
something new about farming, computers, or some new technology.

From the time that he graduated from high school Jean knew his
career vas on the farm, He is the fourth generation on the farm
that has been owned by the Pogu family for over 100 years. In 1995
he received the Century Family Farm Award. In his diversified
operation he grows a variety of crops and runs a feedlot.

Jean is involved in several community organizations and has a
wide range of interests. He once owned a small pony, but his
interest soon shifted to a motorbike. He is an avid skier and was a
member of the ski patrol and a ski instructor for many years. As a
young adult he learned to swim and enjoyed scuba diving. Recently
much of his spare time has been spent at his computer.

Carl joined the Air Force in April 1942 at the age of 18 anda
year later was on his way overseas. He served as a night fighter
pilot with Squadron 409, first on a Beaufighter and then on a
Mosquito. He was stationed in England, France, Belgium end Germany.
During afternoons, in a mischievous mood, he would test his aircraft
by lowering the wheels and bouncing down on French highway:
Cycling factory workers from Lille would scramble into ditches for
safety.

After his discharge in the summer of 1945, Carl worked for the
Provincial Treasury in Regina for over two years. During that tine
hhe met Marie Lanovaz and they were married in October 1946.

Marie, a farm girl, attended La Plaine, a one room country
school, until grade 10 then completed her high school plus a
Commercial Course at Stobart in Duck Lake. In August of 1940 with
determination and a spirit of adventure, Marie travelled to Regina
to work as a secretary for the Saskatchewan Wheat Pool. She was
‘employed there for almost seven years. At that time it was the
Pool's policy to hire only young farm people which made for a lot of
shared interests and lively socializing.

Carl and Marie moved to Duck Lake in December of 1947 after an
offer from Marie's father for Carl to become a partner in the family
store. Lanovaz & Périllat became Lanovaz & Farwell, shortly
afterward this partnership purchased the Service Lumbar Yard from
Arthur Péerillat. Carl eventually took it over and operated it until

162

1973-74. During that period he was also a building contractor and
had projects throughout the province.

In 1948 Carl and Marie bought their first home, a small four
room house, for $800. It was formerly owned by Mr. & Mrs. Arthur
Van Peteghen. The latter was a local midwife for many years. The
Farwells built a house in 1956 in which they resided for 35 years.
They were involved in various community activities, Marie in the
Recreation Club and Curling Club and Carl on the Board of Trade.
Marie also enjoyed crossword puzzles, reading, playing cards,
knitting, boating and visiting. Carl, in recent years, was involved
in the renewal of the cemetery. His interests included boating and
fishing, reading, curling, watching hockey and baseball. Carl and
Marie both died in a fatal car crash in July 1992.

The year I was born we mevad to Duck Lake and my earliest
memories are of the fun time we had in the "old house" as we called
it later. ‘The house was located across from the curling and the
skating rink so during the winter, Jeanne and I spent much time
there. Of course, parts of the summer were spent at Waskesiu, a
place I consider my second home. We also visited Grandma and
Grandpa Lanovaz very often and we especially enjoyed playing games
with Tante Lidie. The Christmas Reveillon get-togethers were also
very memorable.

My eight years of elementary schooling in Duck Lake were
enjoyable ones except maybe for one month in 1958 when Jeanne and I
had to stay at the Convent while Mom and Dad were on a holiday to
the states.

For high school I attended two different colleges. For Grade 9
and 10 I boarded at Notre Dame College in Prince Albert where I
joined air cadets and became a member of the gymnastics club. My
Grade 11 and 12 were taken at College Mathieu in Gravelbourg where I
learned how to play the trumpet and where I performed in a number of
musical productions. Because Gravelbourg was close enough to
Assiniboia where Dad's relatives lived, this gave me an opportunity
to visit my uncles and aunts.

During the summer holidays I worked for dad either at the
lumber yard or on various construction projects in Duck Lake.
Painting was one of the things I had to do and I haven't stopped
painting houses since.

In the fall of 1966 I attended Teacher's College in Saskatoon
and the following year I had a teaching position at La Loche, a
northern Dene community about 500 km fram Saskatoon. I returned to
university in 1968 and during the next few years I taught in the
northern communities of Buffalo Narrows and Besuval. Those years

166

Sharkes Farwell Family
Stacey. Charles, Wade & Diane

|
were probably the most exciting of my teaching career where life-
jong friends were made. I was also able to indulge in one of ny
favourite pastimes, that of fishing.

In 1971 I married Diane Hood, also from Duck Lake, Diane is on
the clerical staff of Holy Cross High School in Saskatoon. We have
two children, Stacey and Wade. Stacey is presently attending
university and Wade is in Grade 8 and a member of the Army Cadet
Corps. Since 1979 I have been living in Saskatoon teaching with the
Saskatoon Separate School system. At the moment I teach at st.
Angela School.

Travelling has always been a passion with me. My wife and I
have been to Hawaii twice, toured the Meritimes and have visited
relatives in California. I've been to Europe twice but the
highlight was the summer of 1994 when Wade and I spent five weeks
touring France, Germany and ending up in London. We spent a week
near La Roche sur Foron visiting the Bouvard relatives and touring
the sites where the Lanovaz family lived. That is something I shall
never forget. Spending time in Normandy on the D-Day beaches vas
another moving experience and it reminded me of Dad's wartime
experiences.

Teaching is always hectic but holidays are a time for
relaxation. I try getting out to the mountains to ski twice a year
and I still enjoy fishing and spending time at Waskesiu. Visiting
relatives whether in Spokane or B.C. or in Duck Lake is something I
am enjoying more and more,

167

I was born on September 23, 1948 in Rosthern, Sask. and grew up
in Duck Lake. I have to admit I was a small package weighing in at
a little over 3 1/2 pounds, but I made up for it by growing inte a
tomboy whe was open to any adventure that my imagination and
circumstances could conjure up. Thank goodness, Mom had been quite
@ tomboy in her day and she rarely put a damper on my activities.
Duck Lake offered many opportunities for adventure. . . lots of
meadows, a favorite stand of trees near my house and the most
tempting of all, a wonderful slough right smack in the middle of
town, abutting the old curling rink. Rafting in the springtime was
one sure-fire way to come home with boots full of water!

Dad made us a skating rink in the yard and Charlie and I
learned early how to get around. It wasn't long before I was the
"tail" in any crack-the-whip game going on in the tovn skating rink
across the road. Broken glasses and one knock-out seemed a minor
price to pay for the thrills and excitement.

In those early years T.V. wasn't common yet so as a family we
Played lots of gamas, both inside (cards, crokinole, chinese
checkers) and outside. One favorite was "anti-anti-I-over" which
probably didn't do much for the roof shingles. Reading was also an
enthusiastic pursuit end Uncle Arthur's Bedtime Stories were a
staple for Charlie and me and later for Julie too.

Besides lots of puddles, spring brought precious marbles out of
their bags. We girls played against the old school or against the
outhouse in the schcol yard. Playing ball was something else to
lock forward to after a long winter. Scrub teams were out every
evening at the school ground. During the "Mois de Marie", May, it

168

Jeannie Farwell riding King

169

was not unusual to see us girls playing in our dresses and good
shoes then plunking hats on our heads and dashing to church for the
prayers.

My schooling started in the old school with all grades in the
building and a very musty basement for lunch hour. The Sisters of
Presentation de Marie were good educators and sticklers for
propriety so the boys got out first and the girls followad five
minutes later (this could be the other way round). A very loud hand
bell kept some semblance of order. "Ici! Ici!" we heard often. 1
enjoyed school and shared the honour of being valedictorian with
Dennis Windels in 1966.

The choir was a wonderful experience that almost all of us had.
I remember how singing the Requiem at funerals would always bring
tears to everyone's eyes, including mine.

I was very lucky to have two farms to spend time at---Pogu's
and Ernest Lanovaz's. My cousins alvays seemed happy to see me, but
I'm not sure that my aunts didn't wonder and worry about what
adventures or misadventures would come out of this visit!! My
memories are so vivid. . .climbing haystacks, catching grasshoppers,
riding the calves. eating chokecherries, riding horses (and the
occasional run away), driving the tractor, making a snow house,
playing in the caboose, milking patient cows and picking eggs from
harried hens; they're endless and make up a very happy part of my
childhood.

Horses were a passion from the time I could sit on the dray
horses ploughing our garden in the spring. Dad surprised me when 1
was 15 with a horse he had bought at an auction. King was a lovely
muscular chestnut colour but had a rather skittery and head-strong
disposition at first. T!'m sure that some merchants and shoppers
remember my early attempts to ride King down main street. Since he
reared at almost every sound or motion I had been given a crude
“bonker" which I applied between his ears every time he reared. I'm
sure it looked like a toss-up as to who had the upper hand! Betty
Lou Urton also had a pony so we spent long summer days exploring the
countryside.

Outhouses were a fact of life for most of us growing up in the
50's. At about age three or four I persuaded Charlie that clothes
were unnecessary. Unfortunately one of us had the bright idea of
stowing them down the outhouse hole. Needless to say we were not in
Mom and Dad's good graces as they fished cut shoes and anything else
they could snag!

The birth of my sister, Julie, just after I turned oleven was a
real joy tome. I finally had a "live" doll to pley with. Julie
put up and I think, enjoyed my mothering and dress-up games. Later
on I put up with what seemed like a hundred dolls and teddies in our
bed. Letters kept us connected after I left home and we've remained
close sisters and good friends.

Family gatherings have been a long tradition and have
contributed to a strong extended family connection. Grandmére and
Grandpére's house (Ferdinand and Ernestine) was the hub for many

170

yeate with summer events also taking place in a variety of places
ancluding the Shrine, Pogu's farm and the river. For those of us
whe have scattered far and wide, these gatherings offer a way to
keep up our family traditions.

then worked in Yorkton, Sask. for two years as a social worker. In

Patrick's School of Social Work; two six month practicums in Toxonto
and Montreal gave me a taste of the East and reaffirmed my love of
the West. Over the next few years I worked in Calgary, Alta. and
Sorrento, B.C. before settling in Victoria in 1976.

My interests over the years have included reading, movies,
Biking, curling, swinming, camping, clay sculpting, roller blading,
gardening and travel. One trip in the summer of 1978 involved
driving and camping across Canada to P.E.I. and back... =
Nonderful way to see this great country. I also made three trips to
Rurope in 1972 (backpacking), 1974 and 1981, This last one was made
with Julie. We stayed for a while at the Mugnier farm in Haute
Savoie (Bouvard relative) and were royally treated. The vieit
established a relationship with Myriam which we have continued to

in February 1994 I entered into what is probably my life's
biggest adventure when I became a mother. I travelled to Haiti to
bring home my daughter, Marisa and during that short trip
encountered a courageous, tenacious and loving people. Marisa was
born on April 25, 1990 and has brought boundless enthisiasn,
Curiosity and affection into my life. she's been involved in
svamming, skating, music, roller blading and T-ball and plans to be
@ "ballerina--figure-skater--actress" when she grows up!
and astigmatism most of my life
ser treatment. In March 1996 I

tasik. The improvement has been dramatic and exciting. A second
treatment in the next few months may result in no glasses. Thet'11
be a day to celebrate!
