I attended stobart Elementary and High School and graduated
from high school in 1977. I worked that summer and one other at the
Duck Lake museum. I enjoyed learning about the history of our area
and meeting and serving our visitors.

In the fall of 1977 I moved to Saskatoon to attend the
Saskatoon Business College where I took a secretarial course. For a
few months following the completion of the program, I worked in the
office of a transport company. In the fall of 1978 I was accepted
in the Personal Development Worker Program at Kelsey Institute.

With this one-year program I succeeded in finding employment with
the Saskatoon Fublic School Board and worked for two years at walter
Murray Collegiate with mentally handicapped teens.

In the summer of 1981 my sister Jeanne asked me to join her on
@ trip to Europe which included visits with many relatives from the
Haute Savoie area. It was an experience I remember fondly for its
wonderful sights, the people we met and the opportunity to visit our
grandparents’ homeland.

After this trip I decided to go back to secretarial vork and
was employed by Wesco/Westinghouse Dist. whera I met my husband,
Brad Evenson. To date, Brad has worked for this company for 22
years, the last 15 years in sales. We were married on August 28,
1982. That fall I wont back to the school board and again worked
with special needs teens at Aden Bowman Collegiate.

Brad was an avid motorcyclist and we took several trips on his
bike before we had children, Some of our most memorable trips
include Victoria & Banff on our honeymoon, Las Vegas, L.A. and
Spokane, and Europe in which we flew our motorcycle overseas with

My first and second
scheol years I attended La
Plaine School one mile
northwest of where we lived.
IT used to sometimes catch
rides with Paul and at other
times Dad or the hired man
would bring me to school.
When we started school in
Duck Lake our transportation
was the three ton truck from
the Lumber Yard driven by
either Uncle Marcel Lanovaz
or Uncle Carl Farwell.

I remember helping Dad
out in the field when he was
baling. He had me driving
the tractor and he had to
run a rope from the rack to
the tractor's hand clutch so
he could stop the tractor as
I was too little to do that
myself. I was five years
old at the time, I've
always enjoyed farming and

176

Working with animals. When I was altar server I attended camps in
Victoire. When I was in my early teens I joined square dancing in
Duck Lake with Dave and Marie Thomas. In i967 1 attended Notre Dame
College in St. Louis.

When I came back to school in Duck Lake I met Angele Guigon,
deughter of Irene Blanchard and Joe Guigon. In October of 1971 we
were married in Duck Lake and took a honeymoon trip to Jasper,
Alberta. When we returned we moved in at Uncle Ernest's old farm
where we lived for four years. In 1975 Mom and Dad moved to town
and we moved to the farm where we are now.

We were blessed with three children, Michelle, Tamuy and Kurt.
Michelle is now living in Saskatoon working and attending U of S in
hopes of acquiring her physical therapist degree. In September of
1994 Michelle travelled to Australia where she worked and toured for
fen months. This was quite an experience for her and for us. Tanmy
will be graduating this year and Kurt is in Grade 9, All of them
have attended Stobart School. They were all active in school sports
and Kurt is also playing hockey with the Rosthern Bantams. They are
all doing well and we are very proud of them.

Angele is very active as an elected trustee for the
Saskatchewan Valley School Division. She also works on the farm
with me and brings the kids to their activities. Summer time keeps
us busy with farming and showing our Black Clydesdale horses. 1
also own two tow trucks; one works all year and the other one I
drive in the winter time in Saskatoon for Astro Towing.

Engaged February 22, 1996

Shoes during the summer were often a non-essential except, of
course, for school and church

We made our sling shots, whistles and stilts. We played
marbles and "knife" and we made a board when cribbage was introduced
to us.

André snd I joined the Duck Lake 4H Club. For me it was a good
learning experience and the cheque from the sale of the calves was a
good incentive. It also became an opportunity to make new friends.

I stayed home for two years after finishing school in La Plaine
and I then went on to Stobart in Duck Lake where I also completed a
business course. I gained a few months of experience at Urton's
Implement Shop and then on to Domremy where I worked for the Bank of
Montreal for a short time. Saskatoon was my next destination and T
worked for the Veterans Land Affairs for several years.

It was while I was in Saskatoon that I had the opportunity to
go to Europe with my aunt Marie-Louise and Uncle Jake (Klassen). I
especially enjoyed meeting the Lanovaz and Bouvard relatives and
experiencing different cultures.

I took advantage of being foot loose and fancy free during
those years and saw different parts of Canada and the United states.

I worked a year in Calgary for Dominion Bridge and I then went
on to Spokane for more adventure. I worked for a farm newspaper and
then a photo finishing company,

Dwayne and his sister Lorraine were born in Plentywood, Montana
vhere their parents ranched. Their families originated in Norway
and Sweden,

After going to university Dwayne came to Spokane where he
worked for Washington Water Power, a utility company. During that
time he also spent two years in the army. He was an internal
auditor for the company during the last twenty years of his career
and retired after thirty-five years.

I worked part time as secretary for St. Mary's Church for abeut
seven years after our children Marc and Carmen ware in school.

We enjoy spending time at our lake cabin which is on Lake Coeur
d'Alene through the summer. I still refer to Duck Lake as "home"
and going there is one of the highlights of the yaar.

Our father René was six years old when he immigrated to Canada
with his parents Francois and Andréanne Lanovaz in 1894.
Our grandparents raised a family of nine children. We still
have two aunts to cherish--Aunt Marie-Louise Klassen, age 101,

living in Saskatoon and Aunt Héléne Daoust, 93 years young, residing

in Montreal.
Our mother Gabrielle was born

in Mareuil Vendée, France. She

was five years old when she came to Canada in 1893 with her parents,

Francois and Marie (Piard) Ripaud and her
We never knew our grandparents Ripaud.

i.

tox Mari

“Louise aged

In the early 1900's our father worked on the railroad in the

‘Dana area.
‘owned four oxen and a horse.

He bought or filed a homestead in the Petersen area and

In 1915 he sold that homestead and purchased land from Mr.

Malone in the La Plaine District.

188

René Lanovaz Family
Lucienne, Louis, Gabrielle & René, Raymond, Maric,
Kneeling: Emile & Henri

René & Gabrielle Lanovaz 1919

Tn 1918 he married Gabriels Ripaud. Shortly after they
Purchased a Model T Ford. Louis and Raymond were born in La Plaine.
The family then moved to the Carlton area whece they lived in a two
story log house,

aoa oa, thet moved back to the La Plaine area. The children
attended Stobart School for the first year of schooling, then they
all went to La Plaine.

» But a good supply of water was obtained. We
electricity, nor did
butter was stored in

Mother made huge gardens and during the summer holidays it had
to be weeded before we each in turn hada weeke holiday.

we heard the steam engine whistle at the railway track.
followed the hay racks, the water wagon
Sock caboose. Mom would help the cook to Prepare the meals for some
25 men.

Naturally Mom had hidden the jug of wine in the cats bin before
the men arrived. This was about 1935-38,

169

In the winter we would go to Mr. Emile Perret to skate and play
hockey. After he left the district Mom and Dad decided we would
have the rink at our place, Dad dug a well for the water supply and
made a slab fence around the rink. Ray and Marie were the ice
makers. Water was hauled by buckets. After school when the chores
were done, all pitched in to flood and look after the ice.
Broomball was also played and to end the season a moccasin dance was
held with generous volunteer music from the area.

After skating coffee and cake was offered. Dad and Mom always
enjoyed having young people around and all were welcome

We will always cherish our good times with our parents and
neighbors.

René Lanovaz Family 1972

Louis was born in 1919.
He married Lucille Boyer in
Duck Lake. They had four
children. one little boy
died in 1959. Two of the
children are married and
have children of their own.
Louis farmed for a few
years before moving to the
town of Rosthern. He was
employed by the town and
Lucille worked at the post
office until her retirement.

Louis and lucille are
very proud of their children
Jand grandchildren.

Louis had joined the
jarmed forces but returned to
help on the farm. He died
in 1991. Lucille lives in
Saskatoon.

December 28, 1968
Lillian (Pelletier) & Gerald Petit

Gerald, Craig, Curtis, Lillian Petit

201

I, Lillian (Pelletier) Petit was born to Marie (Lanovaz) and
Marius Pelletier and am a great granddaughter to Francois and
Andréanne Lanovaz. I married Gerald Petit in 1968 and we were
blessed with two boys, Curtis and Craig. We lived in Saskatoon for
nine years and moved back to Duck Lake in 1977 where my husband and
brother Onil operated a business for eleven years.

Through the years we have enjoyed many family oriented sports
and activities. A few of these were camping, fishing, hunting,
cross-country skiing, downhill skiing, curling, golfing,
trapshooting, ski-dooing and some hockey. The boys have also done
snowboarding and bungee jumping.

Gerald has been very involved with the community with various
organizations such as the Legion, Lions, Nursing Home and the
museum, He has just finished an internship at the Museum of Man and
Nature in Winnipeg and is currently seeking employment.

Curtis, my oldest son, obtained his Bachelor of Fine arts
Degree in the spring of 1995. He enjoys playing the guitar and in
the summers, goes tree planting in B.C.

Craig graduated from high school in 1991 and went to work on
the ski hills of Alberta. He is presently employed with Astro
Towing in Saskatoon, Sask.

I work at the Goodwill Manor Nursing Home as a dietary aide and
call bingo for various groups.

I graduated from Domremy High School in 1975 and spent the next
year taking a secretarial course at Saskatoon Business College. I
met my husband, Allan, in Domromy in 1976, shortly after he moved
fxom Northern Ontario where he grew up as the second youngest of a
family of thirteen children. His ethnic background is Scot, Irish,
and German.

We have been married for 18 years and have raised four
wonderful children: Daniel, Crystal, Adam and Daryl. It's
incredible how quickly the years have gone by! Daniel has graduated
last year from St. Mary High School in Prince Albert. Crystal is
just learning how to drive (yikes!!) and Adam and Daryl are very
active pre-teens.

Allan has been employed at Weyerhauser, the pulp mill in Prince
Albert since April of 1976. I have been back in the work force
fulltime for five years, currently working as a clerk-cashier at
Crescent Heights Pharmacy. We live on an 80 acre spread, 12 1/2
miles north of Prince Albert in the Spruce Homa District. I am
still a farm girl at heart. We have a huge garden, chickens, pigs
(sometimes), and hope to have cattle someday. Our land is seeded
into alfalfa.

Although working, raising children and housework occupy most of
ny time I also enjoy cocking, quilting, cross-stitch, sewing,
gardening, fishing, and family gatherings. Allan is passionately
involved in big-game hunting and fishing. A good part of our house
as adorned with his beautiful animal mounts.

construction company.

He came home most weekends.

In August 1966 we bought a house in Saskatoon and moved there

where three more children were born.

The children are all on their own now.

Dad was born the youngest of six children, to René and
Gabrielle Lanovaz.

Mom was born the second youngest of eleven children to Alfred
and Albina Berubé in Victoria Mines, Ontario.

Dad moved to Copper Cliff, Ontario in 1947 to join hie brother
Raymond. He worked for the Copper Cliff Stanley Stadium for a fev
years and in 1948 he was hired on at the International Nickel
Company (Inco) working in the Smelter Department. He met mom
through friends and apparently would be regularly sitting on her
doorstep until she agreed to date him. They were married on
September 16, 1950. They produced five children, June, Ronald,
Rens, and twins, Jeanette and Claudette.

We resided in Copper Cliff for ten years then moved on to
various locations in Sudbury, Ontario.

Dad had a love for electronics and enjoyed rebuilding and
xepairing television sets, radios, clocks, anything that ticked. He
was very good at recovering anything that needed to be restored to
working order. He also enjoyed reconstructing old furniture to
building new ones. Having experience working at the Copper Cliff

226

Lanovaz Twins
Claudette & Jeanette

Irene (Berubé) & Henri Lanovaz 1983

Avena he made special time and energy into constructing a skating
ink for us children every winter with great success.

Dad was an avid curler. He would bring home many trophies and
érards which he was very proud of. He would keep up his love of
cats sport until the early 1970's when he developed health problems
with his hips

Of course every Saturday night was "Hockey Night in Canada” in
the Lanovaz household. Dad would be glued to the television sec
Cheering on the Boston Bruins. The yelling and outbursts could be
heard throughout the house. He alvaye made cash bets with as
children on who was going te win.

Dad missed his family out west but he gained my mom's family
and believe me, he £it right in.

Dad was @ prankster. He played practical jokes on his
enployees at Inco and with his in-laws. They were always trying to
SUE Go oach other. After all, he was a Lanovaz. He once brought «
marge Zuber snake in his lunch pail to work and terrified everyone!

oe your Purse was a little heavier than usual when Dad was around,
guaranteed he had something to do with it. His sister-in-lawe would
vouch for that,

Dad had a fascination with trains. He had bought my brothers a
yaaan set for Christmas one year and he could hardly wait to arcive
hone from work "to play" with it! Dad was and still is a child at
heart.

227

Henri Lanovaz Family 1990
BAR. Christine Pirs, Ronald Lanovaz, Claudette Lanovaz, Cynthia &
June Pirs, Jeaneatte Steele, René Lanovaz
M.R. Dana, Brian Lanovaz, Seated: Henri & Irene Lanovaz,
ER. Coutney Beresford, Melissa & Cody Lanovaz

Retirement from Inco for Dad was in November of 1984 after 36
years of service. He was 55 years old. He now enjoys camping,
fishing, and spending time with his retired buddies at their
favourite watering hole

Mon was quite a busy mother to her five children, especially
attending to three small children and then a set of twins. Mom's
pastimes when she had the occasional time for herself was reading,
playing cards with friends or from time to time she would work at
banquets in the kitchen at the Copper Cliff Curling Club in the
1960's

Mom was born into a large family of oleven children, all living
an the Sudbury area. She spent a lot of time with her family. We
always had a lot of cousins to play with. We celebrated all the
important holidays together.

Mom and Dad divorced in 1975 but still remain close, Neither
have remarried and each live alone.

Mom is involved with other senior citizens in her building
engaging in card playing, attending dances and participating in pot-
luck dinners. Her love for playing bingo also keeps her occupied
and she visits regularly with her brothers and sisters. She
includes Dad a lot with her visits.

In closing, I would like to state that both my parents were
major designers and molders of the characters we children are today.
I am grateful for their formation and the opportunity to belona

The Lanovaz family have a passion for love, hope, dreams and
togetherness. We will always remain a family unit and for our many
descendants to cone.

I was born the eldest of five children to Henri and Irene
(Berubé) Lanovaz family. I grew up in a small mining town called
Copper Cliff which is about five miles west of the city of Sudbury

My memories of the good ‘ol days in Copper Cliff are still
quite vivid. This little town was a great place to bring up
children. 1 thank my parents for that. We had a very organized
township in tho 1950's and 1960's. The International Nickel Company
of Canada was our main industry and was and still is world wide
known for its nickel producing. Copper Cliff is known for it
"griendly rocks". Actually in the early 1970's astronauts from
NASA, and USA visited Copper Cliff and surrounding areas to explore
our rocks because they have qualities of that of the moon!

230

Dean & June Pirs

Copper Cliff gave us many opportunities and access to the
Copper Cliff Inco Club and various other recreational institutes
owned by Inco where my father was an amployee

Like many other "Hockey Towns" in Northern Ontario skating and
observing our boys master their hockey abilities was a great winter
Pastime, ‘There were skating parties, free skating and Figure
skating at the Copper Cliff Stanley Stadium. I can still smell that
cool scent from the ice when you would walk into the arena.
Remembrance of Dac and Uncle Ray volunteering to clean the ice with
these large shovels are still imprinted in my mind. The majority of
parents always had a hand in some activity or another.

Dad always made an effort every winter to create a
jpumpless" skating rink in our hack yard. This made us children
feel special, but then if you wero a resident of Copper Clifr, you
had to have your own skating sink! God forbid! actually there may

Christine Pirs at the Arrow
Running in the New York Marathon 1993

231

have been some competition there. I'm sure there was some bragging
going on amongst the Inco employees during their lunch breaks. I
know we had the biggest and bumpless rink in all of Copper Cliff!
Thanks to dear ol' Dad.

The summers were spent playing baseball, tennis, golfing and
picnics.

Schooling started for me at the age of six in September of 1957
at the Copper Cliff Public School, One fond memory: when I was in
grade one the teachers felt that I was an excellent reader, so they
nominated me to read over the PA system a welcome speech to our
Governor General Vanier and his wife, from our school and community.
I had presented Mrs. Vanier with flowers and proceeded to shake the
Governor's hand. I was only siz years old. Also, little did I know
that later in life I would again be involved with a member of the
Vanier family.

Acadenics always seemed quite easy for me. Being very
inquisitive and the need for constant and mixed stimuli to occupy my
mind has made me in constant search for knowledge and innovative
ideas. People have often told me that I have this biting wit. My
xesponse, I'm proud to say is, "I'm a Lanovaz, aren't 1?"

So now reaching grade five and skipping on to grade seven wi
gzeat, That meant I'd be in high school in no time. In grade seven
I was the editor of our school newspaper which this position would
later adhere in my later life.

Grade nine and being in high school was a time for new
beginnings for me. I joined the volleyball team, the cheerleading
squad, math club and tutored English. The Beatles were changing the
world and the boys were noticing me. It was 1964.

The sumer of 1965 was fantastic. I had been to visit our
relatives in Saskatchewan when I was a child but this excursion was
most memorable. This time my brother Ron and I joined Uncle Ray and
Aunt Mary on their trip out west. We were going to Duck Lake. I
was to see Lilly and O'Neil (Pelletier) and of course the rest of
the Lanovaz clan. When we arrived at Aunty Marie's home it had
brought back so many memories of Grandma and Grandpa Lanovaz, as
this used to be their home. I remember the wonderful aromas tha!
would work their way around the house from Grandma's excellent
cooking. The smell of the wood burning in her cook stove. The
visualizations of the beautiful table settings with colourful
dinnerware, linen table cloth and home cooked dinners. I'll never
forget Grandma's pantry. Every preserve imaginable was placed
neatly in rows. She made the best rootbeer in the world. Grandma
and Grandpa Lanovaz will never be forgotten.

So when Lilly and 0'Neil greeted us, they had an itinerary
already made out and we were off. We had a beach party, wont to a
teen dance and just hung out. They really knew how to welcome us.

Our visits te Aunty Lucienne and Uncle Lucien's farm were
especially memorable. Aunty’s home cocking was always creative and
from the heart. Her love, laughter, and culinary versatilities were
something I admired and perhaps even inherited. Our journeys to

232

Duck Lake were always celebrated, extraordinary and impressive. I
will always cherish them.

Grades ten and eleven Health Sciences Courecs compelled me to
ponder taking the Registered Nursing Assistant Course. The classes
began in May of 1968. I was seventeen yeara of age. I recall after
writing this exam and being accepted into the course that a friend
and I drove down the street afterward honking the horn and waving
like maniacs to strangers in total exuberation!

Well, I completed and graduated from Nursing in 1970. That
game year I married Dean Firs. We have two daughters, Christine
born April 24, 1967 and Cynthia born July 29, 1970. We were married
only seven short years and later divorced. Dean still remains in
our lives and resides in Sudbury.

Christine graduated in the Fitness Management Program from
George Brown College in Toronto in 1986. She has worked for several
fitness institutes in Toronto. Chris has currently diversified to
working as the account manager for a Food Service and Hospitality
Magazine,, called Kostuck Publication in Toronto. Christine is
single and lives alone. Her career hae her travelling throughout
North America. Christine loves running and has run the New York
Marathon in 1993.

Cynthia graduated from Sudbury Secondary School in 1990 and is
presently in her first year of the Registered Nursing Course at
Cambrian College, Sudbury. Cynthia has accomplished many modelling
indeavours and continues to be creative in building doli houses.

233

My nursing skills included being employed in various
institutions in Sudbury. My preferance is in working with the
physically challenged. I have been employed by an organization
called L'Arche. This association came into existence by a wonderful
God sent human being by the name of "Jean Vanier", the son of
Canada's former Governor General Vanier! He started these croup
homes in France for the disablod and later founded more homes in
North America. Wa teach these God given gifts to become independent
and productive human beings in our society. My love for these
people is unconditional and so rewarding. I also teach literacy and
vooational work. Besides my children they are my life.

Besides working I enjoy arts and crafts, cooking, golfing,
jogging, gardening, cross country skiing. I also manage to do
volunteer work for the Cancer Society and the Diabetes Association.
Come election time I campaign and scrutineer and assist in counting
the ballots.

One of my greatest enjoyments is that I have done some
freelance writing for our local newspaper, "The Sudbury Stax". Some
of my immediate goals are to write children's books, run the New
York marathon with my daughter Christine and to learn how to oil
paint. T've taken a great interest in Canada's Group of Seven, for

inspiration.
Weiting and recreation allow some balance in my life. at this
point, at 44 years of age, my life is just beginning. "I'ma

Lanovaz, aren't I" and proud of it.

I decided I wanted te become independent and to

take the opportunities that were out there in the labour industry.
Little did I know how difficult life could be. Anyway I moved to

age.
In 1974, at the age of 19,

trades. I was fifteen years of

I chose to move on to Saskatoon,
Saskatchewan where my Dad's descendants were residing.

of an overwhelming experience to know and meet my many, many other

My employment in Saskatoon consisted of working for the John

By this time in my life I decided to settle down.

I could

thank my cousin Lillian Lanovaz for introducing me to my future

In 1974 we were married. We have two

Being away from my family in Sudbury we made the decision to

June, Ronald & René Lanovaz

The forest enterprise became my life for thirteen years. In
this period of time I had endeavored downhill skiing, ice fishing
and the enjoyment of the beautiful Scenery. Photography became my
most desired and precious hobby. I wanted to become a hunter but
only with 2 camera. I also have the love of being an artist, a
handyman, and enjoy woodworking, bowling, working with steel, music,
watching wrestling and truck rodeos. 3.C. had a lot of world
wonders consisting of dinosaur tracks vhich totally fascinated me.
There also was an area where I lived where there had been Sasquatch
sitings. I had no intention of proving them right! This area was
out-of-bounds for me!

In the fall of 1978 my health was deteriorating. My doctors
discovered I had a heart murmur. They scheduled surgery and
inserted a mitral valve in my heart. Recovering after six months I
returned to work.

In 1979 my wife Cheryl and I decided to separate and I carried
on with my life the best I could.

i then met Donna Alexander in 1979 and she was to remain in my
life until 1991. We have two children: Melissa, 12 years old and
Cody, 9 years old. They still reside in Chetwynd, B.c.

In the summer of 1991 I enrolled at the Northern Lights College
in Chetwynd, B.C. to pursue my academic carcer in becoming an
electrician. My curiosity from childhood was always the matter of
"What makes things tick". I continued to complete my grade 12 with
great pride. I was 35 yeare old! I realized that an education was
indeed extremely important.

Wmiie attending the college I took up the task of carrying out
Volunteer work for the school. We as students had to participate in
cunning the school. This vas quite fulfilling for me Feeling
productive and just becoming a part of a system, 1 carried cut
confectionery store duties, bookkeeping, participated in cur
theatrical company and my most menorabla task was being Senta Claus
for the students’ children. My own children didn't recognize me.
That meant I was doing a good job.

237

I graduated with honours and great pride at the age of 38 in
duly of 1994,

After graduation I decided to go back east to Brampton,
Ontario, a suburb of Toronto. 1 wanted to apply at a college there
for the electrician course. Money was short so I became employed
with a jacuzzi manufacturer. My career in becoming an electrician
was still an option for me until I became ill in the fell of 1995.
At the age of 39 I had the misfortune of contracting a viral disease
called "Q Virus" which in turn affected my heart. I was
hospitalized for three months. I suffered three cerebral strokes
which have left me legally blind and with the loss of speech. The
doctors performed surgery on my heart to replace the defective
mitral valve.

I am now living with my sister Jeanette (Lanovaz) Steele and
her husband Mark. I have recovered from my surgery but I remain
legally blind with ten percent vision in my left eye. T am
diligently working on my speech with a therapist and looking forward
to what the future holds for me.

I will survive. I have gratitude from many of life's trials
and tribulations. Gratitude is the memory of the heart. They say,
"The greatest pleasure in life is in ‘doing’ what people say you
‘cannot do'". C'est la vie.

As dictated by René Lanovaz
Written by sister, June Pirs

‘The Doucette family came to Duck Lake from the U.S.A. in the

early 1900's.
Julie met Ferdinand p.
an Duck Lake. They were married in 1911 and faemed 10
Places around, where their s.
In 1925 Ferdinand moved hie fani.
Spiritwood. We are told that at one

‘They lived at Mathias Sarazin's, a good friend of the family,
while they built theix log and rough lumber shack this home

phe half section of land he had bought was of poor quality, not
fnitable for grain farming. There was a lot of wild oats, etc. and
this being before the time of chemicals the weeds could not be

245

Julie, Cesile, Hemi
Julie & René Lanovaz, Gnaziclla, vn!

Ferdinand,
Albert

easily controlled. He made hay and grew barley to feed his herd of
cattle. He had some thirty cows that were milked by hand and raised
pigs and chickens. Ferdinand had beautiful bay horses. André had a
beautiful team of grays, Albert had a black mare he called Ford and
@ chestnut, Henry's horse was called Scratch. They were successful
farmers in Spiritwood.

Grandmére was a great cook. She made three full meals a day.
She baked delicious bread and made the best pies and fine thin
crepes. One afternoon Fred Coté and Uncle Henry had gone hunting
and came back with some twenty rabbits. Grandmare mixed the rabbit
meat with pork and Uncle Fred said this was the best hamburger he
ever had. They ate salted pork and potatoes were on their daily
menus. Julie enjoyed cocking. In the morning she made thin crepes
for one and the ordinary pancakes for the other. They had a lot of
vieitors and Grandmére always served them delicious food.

Ferdinand moved his family to Winnipeg except for Cecile who by
now had married Edgar Braconnier. He worked as a cattle buyer at
the stockyards. Times were difficult there and they really
struggled. Again they moved, this time to Studberg near Emerson,
Manitoba. They farmed a half section of land they had purchased and
lived there for five years. The house they built by the river is
still there.

246

Ferdinand & Julie Doucette

Their next move was Fort Frances, Ontaric. They were taking
with them some 200 chickens. The U.S. Custom Officers did not
approve of these birds coming through the border. They were
detained for several hours and this caused some of the chickens to ‘
die before they reached their destination in Fort Frances. There
they farmed, growing crops and raising cattle. Ferdinand also
worked in the bush for a pulp mill.

Ferdinand and Julie both enjoyed gardening, liked to play cards
and loved to be surrounded by their children and grandchildren.
Ferdinand was an honest and hard working man. Julie came west as
often as she could to visit her brothers and sisters in and around
Duck Lake.

André, now 83, still lives in their house on the farm,

Alphonse and Irene built a beautiful home nearby for their
fetixement. Albert and Elsie live half a mile away where they also
farmed and raised their lovely family. Ferdinand passed away in
Winnipeg at 82 from cancer. Julie passed away in Fort Frances at
the age of 93.

Graziella and Fred were married in Spiritwood, Saskatchewan and
settled on Fred's homestead where five of their children were born.

In 1943 they moved to Fort Frances, Ontario. In the summer of
1944 they moved to Winnipeg where Fred found employment as a pipe
fitter. Grace was busy with the tasks every mother has raising a
family.

In 1962 deciding they would enjoy country living they moved to
an acreage in Tyndell, Manitoba where they lived till 1968 when Fred
was ready to retire. They bought their retirement home in st.
Vital, Manitoba where Fred still resides.

In 1991 they had the joy of celebrating their 60th wedding
anniversary amongst their children, grandchildren, many other family
menbers and friends from near and far.

i 1 i Pauline Morin

Frinsatiebo Ss Awnee Edgar & Cecile Braconnier

This is their story, a tale of depression, drought, dreams,
disappointment, failure, hardship, good times, bad times, love and
success.

Edgar and his father Louis each took a homestead in Leoville in
1933. Louis Sr., Edgar and his brothers Ernest and Louis Jr. left
Gravelbourg with magic stars in their eyes. They loaded some
belongings onto wagons and hayracks drawn by their beautiful grey
horses, travelling an approximate distance of 500 mil They slept
under the stars and took turns herding their cattle.

What a difference from the dust storms in Southern Saskatchewan
to the beautiful green forests in Northern Saskatchewan.

284

The first pioneer in Leoville was Leo Charpentier who settled
there in 1927. The village was named after him. The roads were
practically non-existent, just trails chopped through the bush,
zigzagging on high land to avoid rivers, sloughs and swamps. There
were no bridges. To cross some spots was a real nightmare. The
horses and wagons would sink deep in mud holes. Often it was net
prudent to travel. Edgar and Louis cleared the land with an axe and
pulled out stumps, ploughed with horses and picked atones. They
Duilt a small one bedroom shack with logs and fitted the cracks with
grass and mud. It had a sod roof and a dirt floor. A little later
&@ wood floor was added--Edgar's first building to call his home.
There was very little furniture, most of it handmade. Chairs were
stumps with willows for the backs. I recall sleeping on homemade
beds with straw mattresses.

Louis and Agnes had twelve boys and four girls living. Thre
other children had died of the Spanish flu.

fhe family and Edgar stayed in Leoville. at threshing time in order
co .gaF™ Some money Edgar went to work at the Doucetta’s and Feed
otale, mis is where he met Cecile. Her sister Grace wan aarcied
ro Feed Coté. Attar they were married Edgar and Cecile settled oe
Bis farm in Leoville. They grew grain, had horses and raised
cattle.

Mother had a sow called Pearl who produced many litters. Pearl
hes, Very gentle pet and I remember her snorting when Mon came near
her. | When Mom would scratch her with a long stick she would enue
her eyes and grunt. It really was quite cute. Too bed Hollywood
was really unknown to them then. Pearl could have been a atte,

Z xemenber my mother boiling berries to color the whitesach,
When Dad cane home he was not impressed with having pink walle ca
She shack. A fow days lator the walls were green.” hom Sure liked a
challenge.

285

Their daily diet consisted of wild fruit, dried fruit and salt
pork. They ate wild game such as rabbit stew, partridges and other
wild birds, deer and fish. Everything was right at their door.
During the depression years no one had much money. In the winter
Dad would go trapping. He would often take me with him in the
caboose or hayrack drawn by his beautiful horses which he was very
proud of. We would check the trapline for mink, rabbit, muskrat,
beaver, red fox, coyotes and raccoons. He would sell the furs to
buy food and clothing.

Cecile picked and preserved fresh wild fruit such as
strawberries, Saskatoons, raspberries, blueberries, chokecherries,
pincherries, cranberries, all of which grew around. She also grew a
garden, canned vegetables and meats

They were blessed with good crops and gardens. They had sone
milk cows, chickens, a few pigs so that they could trade butter and
eggs at the store for groceries. They sold butter at 15 cents a
pound.

We lived three miles from the village. It was far for me to go
to school on horseback or in a cart and in the winter in a caboose
with a heater in it, In 1948 they built a nice two story home.
Later they moved this house into Leoville. Romeo Laberge's store
had burned to the ground and while they were building the new store
they used our house as a grocery store. Father Grimard was the
priest in Leoville at the time from 1930 to 1952. He
